carried four. 🔵 It is filled with the one opportunity this base has measured that is
genuinely not regional.

🔴 **The opportunity is a defect in how open-source education software states its own
licence, and it is 1 in 10.** Across **84** education repositories read on three channels
this pass, **8 contradict their own documentation** about their grant, and **5 of those are
true contradictions** rather than disclosed bundling. 🔵 Evidence:
[`../evidence/doc-channel.2026-10-30-p554.tsv`](../evidence/doc-channel.2026-10-30-p554.tsv).

🟢 **Why a studio can sell this.** Every client engagement that builds on open source needs
a licence clearance, and the standard way to produce one — read the `LICENSE` file, or trust
the platform's licence field — **is wrong about one repository in ten**, and wrong in the
permissive direction often enough to matter. A clearance that reads **three** channels and
reports disagreement instead of resolving it is a small, concrete deliverable that can be
run in CI on day one of an engagement. 🔵 Recipe: **`P609`**.

🔴 **The shape it finds most often is specific to education**, which is what makes it a
vertical offer rather than a generic one: permissive **code** wrapped around **corpora and
content** under Creative Commons terms — frequently **NonCommercial**. 🔵 Three of this
pass's five true contradictions are exactly that shape
([`learning-commons-org/evaluators`](https://github.com/learning-commons-org/evaluators),
[`learning-commons-org/knowledge-graph`](https://github.com/learning-commons-org/knowledge-graph),
[`CAHLR/OATutor`](https://github.com/CAHLR/OATutor)). 🔵 See `Trend 64`.

🔴 **A limit on this block, stated rather than left to inference:** it is `Global` because
the defect is in publishing practice, not in a jurisdiction. It is **not** a claim that
demand is uniform across the four regions — the four regional subsections above carry that,
and they disagree with each other considerably.


#### 🆕 `p555` — the cross-region artefact is now a PAIR of instruments, and one of them refutes a verdict the other produces

🟢 This base's standing cross-region opportunity is **licence clearance as a billable artefact**.
🟢 **This pass makes it materially stronger, because a clearance report that only reads GitHub's
licence field is wrong on 8 of 79 education repositories — and wrong in BOTH directions.**

| Tool reads… | Gets `OpenOLAT` wrong | Gets `openeducat_erp` wrong |
|---|---|---|
| GitHub licence field alone | 🔴 **yes** — `NOASSERTION` on verbatim Apache-2.0 | 🟢 no — correctly declines |
| A project's own README alone | 🟢 no | 🔴 **yes** — README asserts LGPLv3 |
| **payload + platform + operative-body containment** | 🟢 **no** | 🟢 **no** |

🟢 **The two instruments, both committed this pass:**
[`../ingest/platform-channel.py`](../ingest/platform-channel.py) (joins the platform field to the
three channels `doc-channel.py` reads; `PLATFORM-DISAGREES-WITH-PAYLOAD` = **0 of 79**) and
[`../ingest/operative-body.py`](../ingest/operative-body.py) (separates a **cosmetic**
`NOASSERTION` from a **substantive** one; **9/9 controls**).

🟢 **Measured value of the pair, in one number:** of 8 `NOASSERTION` rows, **3 are cosmetic**
(the artefact is permissively licensed and every scanner says otherwise) and **5 are
substantive**, of which **one is a genuine defect in a 901★ platform** — `openeducat_erp`
shipping ~10 % of the licence text it asserts. 🔵 A clearance engagement that finds three
false alarms and one real defect in a shelf of 79 is **exactly** the result that justifies the
line item, in any region.

🔴 **And the limit, carried so no proposal overstates it:** containment is not governance, the
platform harvest is not scriptable end-to-end (`Gap 555-A`), the operative-body vocabulary covers
**6 families and not CC, BSD, ECL or BUSL** (`Gap 555-B`), and policy primary sources are
unattestable on this session shape (`Gap 555-G`).


#### 🆕 `p556` — the cross-region artefact is an instrument that grades a shelf against a business model

🟢 Delivered this pass and usable in any region:
[`../ingest/delivery-fitness.py`](../ingest/delivery-fitness.py) with
[`../ingest/delivery-fitness-controls.py`](../ingest/delivery-fitness-controls.py) — offline,
no network, reads a harvest TSV and grades every `spdx_id` into `BUILD-ON`, `LINK-ONLY`,
`SELF-HOST-ONLY`, `BLOCK` or `ASK`. 🟢 Run on this pass's 42-address harvest:

| Verdict | n | % |
|---|---|---|
| 🟢 `BUILD-ON` | 25 | 59.5 % |
| 🟡 `SELF-HOST-ONLY` | 11 | 26.2 % |
| 🔴 `BLOCK` | 6 | 14.3 % |

🔴 **40.5 % of the global education shelf cannot be folded into a client deliverable.** 🔵 The
instrument is region-indifferent by construction — a licence does not know where it is — which
is exactly why it belongs in the `Global` bucket and not in any single region's.

🟢 **Two global structural facts this pass establishes:**
1. 🟢 **Velocity and ownership run in opposite directions.** The three largest LMS platforms in
   the world are copyleft; the largest permissive platform (`oppia`, 6 951★, Apache-2.0) is
   smaller than every one of them; and the fastest-growing education agent of the month
   (`amosblomqvist/learnharness`, **4 231★ in 14 days**) is `AGPL-3.0`. **No row gives both.**
2. 🟢 **The tutor is becoming a file.** Seven of the eleven `topic:ai-tutor` repositories above
   200★ ship as agent skills rather than applications (`Trend 62`, `../intel/trends.md#-p556`).
   🔵 Globally that lowers the adaptation cost of a tutoring engagement and raises the relative
   value of the three things a skill cannot contain: the curriculum graph, the eval rubric and
   the LMS integration.

🔵 **The permissive global shortlist, stated once so no region has to re-derive it:** `oppia`
(Apache-2.0) for the lesson engine, `kolibri` (MIT) for low-connectivity delivery, `sakai`
(ECL-2.0) for higher education, `academico` (MIT) for the SIS, `nbgrader` and `otter-grader`
(both BSD-3-Clause) for assessment, `fsrs4anki` + `anki-mcp-server` (both MIT) for retention,
`DeepTutor` (Apache-2.0) or `OpenMAIC` (MIT) for the agent layer, and `gen-mentor` (CC0-1.0,
WWW 2025) as the only peer-reviewed architecture among them.


🆕 **`p557` (`2026-10-30`).** 🔴 **Stop quoting a single market size.** Eight research firms read
this pass: the **2026 base year spans $3.7 B → $11.4 B (3.1×)**, the terminal spans
**$12.3 B → $136.8 B (11.1×)**, and CAGR spans **12.55 % → 40.9 % (3.3×)**. 🔵 The 3.1× is
disagreement about **the present**. 🟢 Standing rule (`557-D`): cite a market size with its
**firm, base year and scope word**, or not at all. 🔵 Narrower segment figures are more honest about
what they measure: **agentic** AI in education **$2.37 B** (2026) → $11.83 B (2031); **AI tutors**
$3.6 B (2026) → $17.7 B (2033).
🔴 **And temper all of it:** a **July 2026 scoping review of 474 studies** finds the research
landscape fragmented, with **pedagogical design still poorly connected to technical capability**.
The market is being sized confidently on an efficacy base that is not settled — that belongs in a
proposal's risk section.
🟢 **Two structural facts for any region.** (1) **The curriculum graph is the scarce asset**
(`Trend 60`, now **confirmed**): the best one in existence is `CC-BY-NC-4.0`, so it is a **priced
build** (`P557-B`), not a dependency. (2) **The LMS category is copyleft-dominated** — of 17 real
LMS addresses swept this pass, **7 are AGPL-3.0, 4 are GPL and 3 are `BUILD-ON`** — and **every
permissive exception is academic** (Sakai `ECL-2.0`, OpenOLAT `Apache-2.0`, RELATE `MIT`). 🟢 For a
product, start from an academic LMS; for a deployment, any of them will serve.
🟡 **And read the distribution, not the core:** `openedx/edx-platform` is Apache-2.0, but
[`overhangio/tutor`](https://github.com/overhangio/tutor) — the thing actually installed — is
**AGPL-3.0**.

### 🟢 `Gap 549-F` — CLOSED, with the field all four regions answered

🟢 **Registered as:** *"one comparable field carried by every region every pass. The
candidate, because all four produced an answer to it this pass, is **what must a product
enforce in code here**."* 🟢 **Adopted, and carried in all four `####` blocks above.**

| region | what a product must enforce **in code** | enforcement unit |
|---|---|---|
| **North America** | per-**district-tenant** policy object; grade gate (AI from grade 9) with **auditable** exceptions for diagnostics and special education | the **tenant** |
| **EMEA** | AI-generated **disclosure at render** (Art. 50, live `2026-08-02`); **prohibited-inference denylist** — emotion recognition a hard block, not a deployer flag; Annex III logging designed now, enforced `2027-12-02` | the **surface** |
| **APAC** | per-jurisdiction classification of each **assessment** feature; compliance clock that depends on **deployment date** (Vietnam `2027-03-01`, +6 months if running before `2026-08-15`); per-provider action-plan **export** | the **(feature × jurisdiction)** pair |
| **LATAM** | **accreditation-grade evidence export** — usage, outcome and review records a quality-assurance body can audit | the **review cycle** |

🟢 **Why this closes `549-F` where the previous field did not.** `p549`'s four blocks carried
a governance-gap percentage, a statutory date, a count of mandating states and a commencement
date — *"each is the best evidence its leg returned and none is the same quantity, so a reader
cannot rank the regions on anything."* 🟢 **This field is the same quantity in all four**: a
buildable requirement, with a named enforcement unit. 🟢 **And it ranks them** — EMEA's is the
only one that **forbids** a capability outright, which is what makes it the binding constraint
on a global product rather than the strictest of four similar ones.

🔴 **It is also immediately load-bearing.** Applied to this pass's own supply, the EMEA row
**disqualifies two of the three best finds** — both biometric attendance systems, MIT-granted
and maintained this quarter. 🟢 **A comparable field that changes a shelf verdict in the pass
that adopts it is not a formatting change**, and that is the argument `549-F` was registered
to make. Verdict band: **`Gap 551-C`**; mitigation recipe: **`P604`**.

instruments this base already holds, more precisely than the channel states them, is a

### 🟢 `Gap 550-F` — the demand legs have hit their ceiling, and the next instrument is not a search

🔴 **Registered this pass.** Four regions × eleven passes of the same four prescribed
queries has converged. 🔵 What is *not* ingested is the layer underneath: **ministry
procurement documents, national observatory raw instruments, and the AI policies the
governed minority have actually published** — LATAM's `26 %`, Europe/NA higher ed's
`70 %`. 🟢 **Those are primary, they are not in the search channel's index, and they are
the exact artefacts a governance engagement is sold against**: a client does not buy a
summary of the governance gap, it buys a policy that survives its own regulator.

🔴 **This base cannot currently reach them** — `eur-lex` is a gateway policy denial
(`Gap 500-D`, open since `p500`), and `p548`'s unreachable-primary-host pattern has three
members. 🟢 **Stating the ceiling is what makes the next pass's choice a choice**, rather
than a twelfth identical sweep that returns the same instruments.

### 🟢 The supply side moved, and it moved a region

🟢 [`ilios/ilios`](https://github.com/ilios/ilios) — **MIT**, **88★**, **560 releases**,
`v3.141.1`, 14 years maintained, **243 open issues**, pushed `2026-10-29`, filed on
[`../verticals/solutions.md`](../verticals/solutions.md#-p550--2026-10-30).

`Copyright (c) 2017 **The Regents of the University of California**`, read as bytes from
the licence payload, with the README naming **UCSF**. 🔵 **Shelf rule 18 paid for itself
here:** the grant was graded first and it settled placement **for free**, on a pass where
the other promotion (`Clew`) consumed a region question and closed it as `UNPLACED`.

🔴 **Why it matters commercially more than its star count suggests.** Every other
institutional platform on this base is copyleft — Moodle GPL-3.0, Open edX AGPL-3.0,
OpenEduCat LGPL-3.0, RosarioSIS and openSIS GPL-2.0. 🟢 **`ilios` is the only one a client
can fork without a copyleft conversation**, and its model — longitudinal curriculum,
competency mapping, instructional hours, accreditation reporting — is the substrate the NA
buyer's non-discretionary spend already sits on. 🔵 `Trend 6` (*the sector is copyleft,
and that decides the architecture*) now has its first documented exception, and the
exception is the commercially useful one.

### 🔴 `Gap 549-F` — the regional opportunity blocks are not comparable, and that is a spec defect

🔴 **Measured on this page:** the `#### p549` subsections above carry a **governance-gap
percentage** for LATAM, a **statutory date** for EMEA, a **count of mandating states** for NA
and a **commencement date plus a per-provider deliverable** for APAC. 🔵 Each is the best
evidence its leg returned, and **none of them is the same quantity**, so a reader cannot rank
the four regions on anything.

🟢 **Registered as `Gap 549-F`:** the region block needs **one comparable field** carried by
every region every pass — the candidate is *"what must a product enforce in code here"*,
because all four regions produced an answer to it this pass (NA: per-grade gating and an
appealable detection decision · EMEA: conformity assessment and human oversight on an
Annex III system · APAC: a per-institution risk plan and a per-country privacy floor ·
LATAM: a per-country compliance surface with no common definitions). 🔵 **`p549` does not
add the field**, because adding a field to the authoritative block is a spec change that
belongs beside `547-G` and `548-E` rather than inside one pass's section.

### EMEA

🟢 **EMEA is where the regional picture actually moved this pass, and it gained a tier this
page did not have.** `p455` established the EMEA inversion for systems of record for
**administration** — PRONOTE, EcoleDirecte, Schulportal Hessen, WebUntis: proprietary,

record for CONTENT**, and they do not behave the same way:

| Publisher platform | Offline protection | Lawful path for a Globant engagement |
|---|---|---|
| **BiBox 2.0** (Westermann) | 🔴 AES-256-CTR | 🔴 **None at any licence** — reading the content is the regulated act (**UrhG § 95a**, **InfoSoc 2001/29/EC Art. 6**) |
| **Cornelsen Offline Lernen** | 🔴 AES-128-CBC | 🔴 **None**, same basis |
| **Klett Lernen** | 🟢 **unencrypted** | 🟡 **Open technically; licence the corpus** — an ordinary commercial negotiation with a named counterparty |

🔵 **That one-of-three is the opportunity.** The engagement is **licence the Klett corpus,
then build the agent layer on a curriculum ontology already held and permissive** —
`oaknational/oak-open-curriculum-ecosystem` (MIT, 6 835 commits, MCP server over national
curriculum data) and `esco-skill-extractor` (MIT, EU ESCO taxonomy) — **neither of which
requires a publisher at all**.

🔴 **And a procurement risk class EMEA buyers should be handed directly.** The best German
artefact this pass found declares **MIT in `plugin.json` and in its README while serving no
licence file at any path**, under the holder `{"name": "anonym"}` — so MIT's notice clause
cannot be satisfied even in principle (`Gap 547-A`). 🔵 **A German state procurement that
reads a manifest field as a grant would conclude this is MIT.** It is not usable, and the
second reason is worse than the first (`Gap 547-B`).

🟢 **A requirement, not an asset, and it will be asked for on day one:**
`Förderschwerpunkt` differentiation (ESE · Hören · Lernen) **crossed with subject** — a
dimension **no platform on [`../verticals/solutions.md`](../verticals/solutions.md)
models**.

### APAC

🟢 **APAC gains the one promotable artefact of the pass, and the reason it is promotable is
regulatory fit rather than popularity.**
[`Swon010191/Math_teacher`](https://github.com/Swon010191/Math_teacher) — **MIT**, grant
file read first-hand (**1 066 B**), 42 commits, Vietnam — carries two properties that are
exactly the mitigations a high-risk regime demands:

1. 🟢 **The mathematics is deterministic, not generated** — equations solved exactly and
   **verified by substitution**; the LLM never produces the answer.
2. 🟢 **A teacher-approval gate** — *"giáo viên duyệt trước khi lên bảng"*; AI suggestions
   reach the board only after a teacher passes them.

🔵 **Why that is a market fact and not a code review.** **Vietnam's Decision
33/2026/QD-TTg** places education AI on its high-risk list, and this base already holds
**Korea's AI Basic Act** (in force `2026-01-22`) and **TEQSA**'s institutional action-plan
requirement in Australia. 🟢 **An APAC engagement can therefore start from an MIT artefact
whose architecture already encodes the human-oversight and determinism arguments**, rather
than retrofitting them to a chatbot. It also runs **local-first with no API key**, which is
what makes it deployable under a data-protection regime rather than only demonstrable.

🔴 **The honest limit:** stars are **UNREAD** (`Gap 547-E`), last commit `2026-09-17`, and
the open activity-interchange format is still marked *giai đoạn sau*. 🔵 This is a **starting
point to own and extend**, not a dependency to adopt.

### LATAM

🟢 **LATAM produced the most substantial new artefact of the pass and cannot use it.**
[`ceanjonnube-negra/docente-ia`](https://github.com/ceanjonnube-negra/docente-ia) —
**245 commits**, last `2026-10-28` — is **Mexico-placed on first-hand evidence**, not on its

and `PROJECT_MAP.md` carries the schema flag `es_sep`. 🔴 **It carries no grant in its tree
and declares none anywhere.**

🟢 **Its architecture is the transferable asset even though its code is not:** *"el Tool
Registry determinista … **nunca pasa por Claude**"* — a deterministic tool registry held
deliberately apart from LLM function-calling, plus **official-source restriction by
domain**. 🔵 **That second idea is the one to carry into any public-sector education
engagement in the region**: an agent that answers curriculum and calendar questions only
from `.gob.mx` is answerable to a ministry in a way a general web search is not.

🔴 **A second Chilean artefact is rejected on licence, first-hand:**
[`francisco-sereno/guia-fundamentos-IAGen`](https://github.com/francisco-sereno/guia-fundamentos-IAGen)
(Universidad de Chile, VTI) states **CC BY-NC-SA 4.0** and its prose extends the licence to
*"este material educativo **y su código fuente**"* — 🔴 **NonCommercial reaches the code**,

🟢 **The action this region has had prescribed since `ACTION p417-A` is now the highest-value
move on the whole base, and `p547` makes the case numerically:** **ask for a licence.** Of
six new systems this pass, **three are ungranted and otherwise shelf-eligible** — one with
245 commits. 🔵 A single `LICENSE` commit moves each from unusable to shelf-eligible, and

### 🔴 The four-region demand sweep — saturated, and recorded claim by claim

🔴 **Every claim the four mandated regional legs returned is already on this base.** Named
so the result is checkable rather than merely asserted, with the one exception marked:

|---|---|---|

🔵 **This is the seventh consecutive demand saturation and the eighth on supply.** 🟢 **The

search, and the two candidates this pass actually proved work are the **git protocol**
(273/273 dates, 96 s) and **`raw.githubusercontent.com`** (64/64 payloads).

### 🟢 `Gap 500-D` — the diagnosis is upgraded from inference to the gateway's own log

`Gap 500-D` has been open since `p500`: the claim that the *Digital Omnibus* is
**Regulation (EU) 2026/1744** is single-sourced to vendor pages because
`eur-lex.europa.eu` does not resolve, so **the steward's own text has never been read by
this base.** 🔴 **Still true at `p546`** — but the reason is now attested rather than
inferred.

Two `eur-lex` URLs were requested and both returned `HTTP 000`. The session gateway's own
status endpoint then named the cause, verbatim:

> `"kind": "connect_rejected"`,
> `"detail": "gateway answered 403 to CONNECT (policy denial or upstream failure)"`,
> `"host": "eur-lex.europa.eu:443"`

🟢 **So this is a network-policy denial at the gateway, not a DNS failure, not a
`eur-lex` outage and not a transient.** 🔵 **That distinction is the whole value of the
upgrade: it converts an open gap into a request someone can grant.** The remedy is to
allow `eur-lex.europa.eu` in this environment's network policy; nothing in the codebase
can fix it. 🔴 **Until then every AI Act date on this page remains vendor-sourced**, and
`2026/1744` is held on secondary corroboration only.

### 🟢 The GitHub API is an authorisation scope, not a proxy block — and this changes the ask

🔴 **This base has recorded the GitHub API as proxy-blocked for many passes.** 🟢 **It is
not.** `gh`, which is pre-configured for this session's proxy, was pointed at a public
repository and the API answered — with a **403 whose body names the cause and the
remedy**:

> *"GitHub access to this repository is not enabled for this session. Use `add_repo` to
> request access."*

🔵 **The request reached GitHub and GitHub replied.** The transport works; the
**authorisation scope** excludes repositories this session is not attached to. 🟢 **Why
the distinction is worth a section:** a proxy block is nobody's to grant, while a scope
is — and it tells this base exactly which instruments are and are not buildable here.

🔴 **It also sets a boundary this pass respected rather than worked around.** Attaching
sixty-five third-party repositories to read one metadata field each would mint
credentials against repositories Globant does not own. 🔵 **Not done, and not
recommended.** The consequence is recorded instead: the GitHub **About** line has **no
byte-exact channel** in this environment, which is what bounds the quote gate on
[`../agents/trending.md`](../agents/trending.md#-p546--2026-10-30).

| Channel | Verdict at `p546` | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| `raw.githubusercontent.com` | 🟢 **200** | 64/64 payloads fetched |
| git smart-HTTP (`ls-remote`, blobless clone) | 🟢 **works** | 273/273 dated, 0 failures |
| `github.com` HTML via **WebFetch** | 🟢 **200** | two trend digests read in full |
| `github.com` HTML via `curl` | 🔴 **403** | 3/3 repositories |
| `api.github.com` via `curl` | 🔴 **403** | 4/4 — body is GitHub's, not the proxy's |
| `api.github.com` via `gh` | 🔴 **403 — scope, remedy named** | see above |
| `eur-lex.europa.eu` | 🔴 **`connect_rejected`, gateway policy** | gateway log, quoted above |

### Opportunities by region — `p546` (historical; the authoritative block is the single `##` at the top of this page)

🔵 **Each region below is placed from the evidence this pass actually produced, and the
instrument findings are region-neutral, so they apply to all four equally.** The
succession rule — *pick a standards implementation by its last commit date, not by whose
logo is on it* — is the one finding here that a studio can act on in any geography.

### North America

🟢 **The strongest opportunity this pass surfaces anywhere, and it is a maintenance fact
rather than a market fact.** The **Ed-Fi Alliance** stack is the most actively maintained
interop family on this base — `Data-Management-Service` **0 days**, `Ed-Fi-ODS` **1 day**,
`Ed-Fi-AdminApp` **1 day**, `edfi-oneroster` **13 days**, `Fiona` **1 day** — and Ed-Fi is
a **US K-12 data standard**. 🔵 A North American engagement can therefore start from a
living data layer rather than building one, which is not true of any other region here.
🔴 **The regulatory shape is 134 bills across 31 states with four states mandating
district-level policy**, so the deliverable that sells is **policy-conformant
auditability**, not capability.

### EMEA

🟢 **Sequencing is the whole argument, and it is unchanged and still correct:** Article 50
transparency duties are **live now** (`2026-08-02`) while Annex III high-risk obligations
for stand-alone education systems do **not** bite until **`2027-12-02`**. 🔵 So a
transparency-and-marking layer is billable today and a conformity-assessment programme is
a 2027 engagement that can be sold now. 🔴 **The demand leg is a measured blank for the
seventh consecutive pass** — queried for EMEA adoption and players it returns US sampling
frames; the one new figure (**USD 2.64 B**, Finland/Estonia/Netherlands leading) is
recorded **unverified**. 🟢 **Supply, by contrast, is EMEA's strength:** `oak-open-curriculum-ecosystem`
(England, state-funded, **8 days** fresh, 6 835 commits) is the maturity signal this
region lacked, and it survives `p546`'s date field where two of its sibling promotions
did not.

### APAC

🟢 **The largest open-source education agents on any shelf are APAC-origin and both are
current** — `DeepTutor` (HKU) and `OpenMAIC` (Tsinghua), each with an October 2026
release. 🔴 **And the compliance surface is the most fragmented of the four regions:**
Korea's AI Basic Act in force since `2026-01-22`, Vietnam's Decision 33/2026/QD-TTg naming
automated assessment high-risk, China's registration and labelling duties, Australia's
TEQSA action plans, and Singapore/Australia/NZ/Malaysia sectoral rather than standalone.
🔵 **The engagement shape that follows is a policy-parameterised deployment** — one
architecture, per-jurisdiction policy nodes — because no single APAC regime generalises.

### LATAM

🟢 **The institutional scaffolding arrived this year and is named**: UNESCO's Regional
Observatory on AI in Education, **Santiago, 14 Apr 2026**, with CAF, CENIA, Cetic.br,
ECLAC, Fundación Ceibal, Fundación Santillana, Tecnológico de Monterrey, ProFuturo,
Universidad del Desarrollo and IRCAI. 🔴 **And the governance gap is the opportunity,
quantified:** **87 %** of 200 surveyed institutions across 19 countries use AI in at least
one area while only **26 %** have a formal strategy, and **716** AI instruments across 23
countries means fragmentation rather than coverage. 🟢 **Supply is curriculum-anchored and
Brazilian**: three of the four curriculum-anchored agents on the whole shelf anchor on the
**BNCC** (`agente-bncc-professores`, `Jeanikt/tutor-ai-agent`, `bncc-dev/bncc-benchmark`).
🔴 **Which is also the warning** — see the anchor census on
[`../intel/trends.md`](trends.md): a curriculum anchor is **non-portable by
construction**, so a BNCC-anchored asset does not travel to the other three regions.

# Education AI — Market Map and Opportunities

**Pass `p545` · 2026-10-30.** Figures are attributed to the source that produced
them. Where sources disagree the disagreement is shown rather than averaged away,
because an averaged number is a number nobody published.

> 🟢 **`p545` adds the one band this page never had: a sizing of the *agentic* segment
> by itself.** Every figure this base carried — `9.58B`, `42.48B`, `136.79B` — sizes **AI
> in education entire**. 🟢 **Mordor Intelligence** prices *agentic AI in education and
> learning technologies* at **USD 2.37 B (2026) → USD 11.83 B (2031)**, and **Grand View
> Research** prices *AI tutors* at **USD 2.1 B (2025) → USD 2.7 B (2026)**, CAGR **30.5 %**
> to 2033. 🔴 **The two overlap and are not additive** — different firms, different
> definitions, and neither says where "agentic" stops and "tutor" starts. 🔵 **The usable
> read is the ratio, not the number: on Mordor's own definition the agentic segment is
> roughly a quarter of a whole-market band that the firms themselves put between
> `USD 6.4 B` and `USD 11.4 B` for 2026.** Mordor also states the go-to-market shape
> plainly — *tutoring remains the gateway purchase that seeds broader platform
> penetration*.
>

> a sixth consecutive saturation on the demand side, now matching the supply side's

> district deadline, the **SUNY** systemwide policy, NYC's March-2026 guidance and June

> 🔴 **And one figure is recorded only to be refused:** a secondary aggregator claims

> **No primary source, no methodology, no named counter. It is not adopted**, and it is

## Global market size — four firms, one wide band

| Source | 2026 | Forecast | CAGR |
|---|---|---|---|
| Precedence Research | **USD 9.58B** | USD 136.79B by 2035 | 34.52% (2026-2035) |
| Resourcera | USD 10.4B | USD 32.27B by 2030 | — |
| HolonIQ | USD 12.3B (2026, global) | — | — |
| Range across firms (roundup) | **USD 6.4B – 12.3B** | ~USD 32B by 2030 (low end) | ~30-35% cluster |

**Read the band, not the point.** A ~92% spread between the low and high 2026
estimate means the methodology is doing more work than the market. What the
sources agree on is the *growth rate* (30-35%) and the *regional ranking*, and
those are the usable parts.

Regional share: **North America ~36%** (largest), **APAC fastest-growing at
35.3% CAGR**.

### 🆕 Regional sizing from one firm, one method (`p529`)

Every table above mixes firms, so regional comparison across them is arithmetic
on incompatible definitions. These three figures come from **MarketsandMarkets'
per-geography AI-in-education reports** — same publisher, same method, so the
*ratios* are meaningful even if the levels are vendor estimates:

| Region | 2024 | 2029 forecast | Implied CAGR |
|---|---|---|---|
| **North America** | USD **951M** | ~USD **2.3B** | ~15.9% |
| **APAC** | USD **591.6M** | USD **1,848.1M** | ~20.9% — *named fastest-growing* |
| **EMEA (Europe)** | USD **512.6M** | USD **1,328.8M** | ~21% — UK, Germany, France lead |
| 🔴 **LATAM** | ⛔ **not published** | ⛔ **not published** | ⛔ — |

🔵 **Read the ratio, not the level.** NA is roughly **1.6×** APAC and **1.9×**
Europe today, and APAC closes the gap but does not overtake inside the window.
🔴 **The LATAM row is the finding.** The firm that sizes the other three regions
does not size LATAM — so every LATAM market figure this base holds comes from a
*different* methodology than its NA/EMEA/APAC figures, and the regional
comparisons in this file are **not like-for-like for LATAM**. Stated here so the
limitation travels with the numbers.

⚠️ These are **lower** than the global tables above imply, because the global
figures include corporate training and consumer learning that the per-geography
reports scope out. Do not add these rows to a global total.

## Adoption — better evidenced than market size

| Signal | Figure | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Students using AI in their studies (16 countries) | **86%** | Digital Education Council, 2025 |
| University students using AI | 92% (up from 66%) | K12 Academics, *State of AI in Education 2026* |
| K-12 teachers using generative AI for work | **25% → 53%** in one year | RAND, 2025 |
| K-12 students using AI for school | 54% | RAND, 2025 |
| Time saved by weekly teacher AI users | **5.9 hours/week** | Gallup + Walton Family Foundation, 2025 |
| US teachers using AI tools | 60% | Child Trends / US Dept. of Education analysis |
| 🆕 Higher-ed institutions in **Europe + North America** with AI guidance in place or in development | **70%** | regional overview, 2026 |
| 🆕 LATAM higher-ed institutions using AI somewhere | **87%** | **UNESCO IESALC + UNU-IAS**, 200 institutions / 19 countries |
| 🆕 …of those, with a **formal AI strategy** | **26%** | same study |
| US public schools with formal AI policies for students | "most still lack" one | Child Trends / US Dept. of Education |
| Schools with rules governing AI use | **under 10%** | roundup, unattributed to a primary |

**This gap is the commercial thesis, and the UNESCO pair now quantifies it
precisely.** 87% using, 26% with a strategy — a 61-point governance deficit, from
a named primary with a stated sample. Adoption is near-universal among students
and past half among teachers, while governance is near-absent. The scarce good is
not a tutor — it is a **defensible, auditable** tutor. Every regional opportunity
below is a variation on that sentence.

⚠️ *The "under 10%" figure appears in a secondary roundup without a primary
citation. Treat it as directional. The RAND, Gallup, Digital Education Council and
UNESCO IESALC figures carry named primaries and can be quoted to a client. The
X-Pilot report's headline statistics (42% learning-outcome gain, 83% planning
AI-TA deployment) are vendor-blog figures — **do not quote them.***

## 🆕 `p532` — the four regional legs, reported per region including the blanks

🔵 **Why this section exists at all.** An unstated blank reads exactly like
coverage. All four regional legs were run this pass; **none returned a fact this
base does not already hold**, and two returned a genuine void. Printing that is
the finding — it tells a successor which channels are saturated and which are
simply empty.

| Region | What the leg returned this pass | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **North America** | State K-12 AI policy (Idaho SB 1227, Maryland SB 720, Oklahoma SB 1734; NY A 9190 and California AB 1159 pending), NYC traffic-light guidance with a red tier barring AI from grading/discipline/placement, federal H.R. 8747 on AI literacy funding | 🔴 **Saturated — nothing new.** 🟡 Trackers disagree on the state count (28+DC vs 34+DC+PR) and on enacted-vs-introduced status, so **no specific figure here is quotable without a primary legislature source**. |
| **EMEA** | **Regulation (EU) 2026/1744** moving stand-alone high-risk education obligations from `2026-08-02` to **`2027-12-02`**; Art. 50 transparency duties still from `2026-08-02`; emotion recognition in schools prohibited since `2025-02-02` (penalties to €35M / 7% turnover) | 🔴 **Saturated — nothing new**; `2026/1744` is already across six files here. 🔴 **The DEMAND leg is a measured blank for the second consecutive pass** — queried for EMEA adoption and players it returned US-only survey bases and a 2023-edition study. 🟢 Its **supply** leg is the strongest of any region: `get-it` (Milan) is this pass's one *stated*-region shelf row. |
| **APAC** | **Vietnam Decision 33/2026/QD-TTg**, which names **education among six high-risk sectors** (automated assessment, behavioural monitoring) — the region's clearest education-specific AI rule; Japan promotion-not-penalty; Ipsos Education Monitor 2026 support for banning AI in schools, **23% Indonesia → 31% South Korea**, Japan rising 21% → 29% | 🔴 **Saturated — nothing new.** Both the Vietnam decision and the Ipsos series are already held. |
| **LATAM** | OECD secondary-teacher AI use — **Brazil 56%, Chile 55%, Colombia 53%, Costa Rica 52%** against an OECD average of **36%**; UNESCO: **87%** of regional universities use AI, **26%** have a strategy, **9%** evaluate it; in Brazil **53%** of administrators use AI and **22%** have guidelines; Google.org **$4.6M** across nine countries targeting 1.25M students by 2028; Teachy (BR), Ginia (MX) | 🔴 **Saturated — nothing new.** 🔴 **The regulation sub-leg is a genuine void:** asked directly for Brazilian and Mexican AI-in-education rules, search returned **PL 2338 still pending in the Chamber** (*aguardando parecer do relator*, mid-Oct 2026, **no education-specific provision**) and **nothing whatsoever for Mexico**. |

🟢 **The one durable regional conclusion, and it is unchanged:** the region with
the **highest adoption** (LATAM, teachers 16–20 points above the OECD average)
is also the one with the **least institutional scaffolding** (9% of universities
evaluate their own AI use) and **no binding education rule in its two largest
markets**. 🔵 That inversion is the LATAM engagement thesis and it has now
survived four passes of re-checking — which is a stronger statement than any new
number this pass could have added.

## 🆕 `p533` — the four regional legs re-run: **saturated on demand, moving on supply**

🔵 **The result is a fifth consecutive pass of the same shape, and it is now a
finding about the channel rather than about the window.** All four regional legs
were run at `2026` (computed, not hardcoded), plus all four global legs. 🔴 **The
demand and regulation legs returned exactly one figure this base did not already
hold.** What *did* move this pass is **supply** — five shelf rows and eleven
foundation rows, three of them regionally placed — and none of it came from a
market query.

| Region | What the leg returned | New? |
|---|---|---|
| **North America** | 134 AI-in-education bills across 31 states in 2026; Oklahoma S.B. 1734 (written district AI policy before 2027-28; AI barred as the primary basis for grading, discipline or placement); Maryland's *AI Ready Schools Act* (24 districts, 120 days from MSDE guidance); Utah H.B. 218 (grade 7–8 digital-skills course incl. AI literacy); H.R. 8747; Gallup ~60% of teachers using AI | 🔴 **No** — all held, and the bill-count disagreement `p532` flagged is **unresolved**: this pass got *134 bills / 31 states* where `p532` held *28+DC vs 34+DC+PR*. 🟡 Still not quotable without a primary legislature source. |
| **EMEA** | EU AI Act high-risk classification for systems that evaluate learners, assign them to programmes, proctor assessments or gate qualifications; Digital Omnibus timing; extraterritorial reach to non-EU vendors; Gulf movement (UAE MoE, KHDA); UK light-touch via existing regulators; OECD *Digital Education Outlook 2026* urging purpose-built over general-purpose educational AI | 🔴 **No** — all held. 🔴 **Sources again disagreed on the high-risk date** (one gave 2026-08, another 2027-12 standalone / 2028-08 embedded); this base's **Regulation (EU) 2026/1744** reading stands and the conflict is a source-quality note, not a correction. 🔴 **The DEMAND leg is a measured blank for the third consecutive pass.** |
| **APAC** | Korea **AI Basic Act** in force 2026-01-22 with a one-year penalty grace period; **Vietnam** Decision naming education a high-risk sector, compliance by 2027-03-01 (**+6 months for education systems already running before 2026-08-15**); China algorithm filings + synthetic-content labelling; Singapore AI Verify; Japan/Taiwan drafting; Australia preparing high-risk guardrails | 🔴 **No** — held, the Vietnam decision included. 🔴 **No K-12 or higher-ed APAC *adoption rate* exists in any result, for the second pass running** — the region's regulation is well documented here and its demand is not. |
| **LATAM** | 🟢 **UNESCO IESALC (Sept 2026): 200 institutions, 19 countries — 87% using AI in at least one area and 🆕 `74%` using it in teaching and learning specifically**, against **26%** with a formal AI strategy; UNESCO **Observatory on AI in Education for LAC** launched April 2026 at ECLAC, Santiago; TALIS 2024 teacher use Brazil 56% / Chile 55% / Colombia 53% / Costa Rica 52% vs OECD 36%; Uruguay's **Ceibal** | 🟡 **One new figure: `74%`** — the teaching-and-learning sub-rate inside the 87% headline this base already held. 🔵 It matters because it separates *administrative* adoption from adoption **in the classroom**, which is the half an engagement is sold against. |

🟢 **The durable conclusion survives a fifth re-check and gains a sharper
number:** LATAM has the region's highest measured classroom adoption (**74%** of
surveyed institutions, teachers 16–20 points above the OECD average) and the
least scaffolding (**26%** with a strategy, **9%** evaluating). 🔴 **And the
inversion now runs the other way on evidence quality too:** LATAM is the
*best-measured* region on this base — a 200-institution, 19-country instrument
with a published method — while **EMEA and APAC demand remain blanks**. The
region usually treated as the under-evidenced leg is the only one with a census.

## 🆕 `p534` — the four regional legs re-run: **one region moved, and it moved on supply**

All four regional legs and all four global legs were run at `2026` (computed from
the system date, not hardcoded). 🔵 **The headline is a reversal of the last six
passes' shape:** the leg that produced something was **North America**, and what
it produced was **code**, not a figure.

| Region | Supply (addresses) | Demand / regulation | New? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **North America** | 🟢 **4 permissive addresses**, all institutional: [`mitodl/learn-ai`](https://github.com/mitodl/learn-ai) (MIT, 816 commits, **0★**), [`mitodl/agent-kit`](https://github.com/mitodl/agent-kit) (BSD-3), [`Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/Fiona`](https://github.com/Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/Fiona) + [`AI-Tools-for-Ed-Fi-SDLC`](https://github.com/Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/AI-Tools-for-Ed-Fi-SDLC) (both Apache-2.0, both 0★) | **36 %** of global market share; 🔴 **~10 %** of institutions with formal AI guidelines; 🔴 **71 %** of US teachers without AI training; *"a relative regulatory vacuum — no equivalent to the FDA for educational technology"*, with state-level piecemeal requirements (**Colorado, Texas**); Canada's **AIDA** 🔴 **not confirmed as law** in anything this leg returned | 🟢 **Supply: yes, 4.** 🟡 Demand: the three percentages are new to this base but come from a **statistics roundup that does not name its underlying data** — carried as secondary |
| **EMEA** | 🔴 **0** | 🔴 **Zero education-specific results — second consecutive pass.** The leg returns enterprise-AI material: the **EU AI Act** as the adoption driver, CompTIA's 2026 skills outlook (🔴 whose survey is **1 012 US professionals**, so not EMEA data at all), Workday's **2023** study (60 % siloed data), Bandwidth/Cavell Feb 2026 on compliance complexity | 🔴 **No.** And the finding is about the **query**, not the region — see declared gaps |
| **APAC** | 🔴 0 | **TCS × Pearson** multi-year AI learning alliance for employer skills gaps; **LearnUpon** opens a Sydney HQ with AI course authoring; **OpenAI appoints an ANZ policy lead** as Canberra tightens AI governance and copyright; 🔴 Singapore's named consultations are **financial-sector**, not education; one forecast puts APAC as the **fastest-growing region at 24.8 % CAGR (2026–2033)** | 🟡 **Commercial motion, no education-specific regulation.** The regulation this leg returned last pass (Korea's AI Basic Act, Vietnam's high-risk designation) did **not** reappear — treat its absence as a channel artefact, not a repeal |
| **LATAM** | 🔴 0 | 🟢 **A second independent instrument, and it agrees with the first on the shape.** UNESCO **IESALC** (with UNU-IAS): 200 institutions, **19 countries**, **87 %** using AI in ≥1 area, 🔴 **26 %** with any formal framework. 🆕 **Digital Education Council**, with **Tec de Monterrey** and its Institute for the Future of Education: **92 % of students** and **79 % of faculty** actively engaging, 🔴 **~30 %** of LATAM universities with a published AI policy. Chile is furthest along (national AI policy since **2021**, bill in discussion); **Brazil and Colombia** have national strategies but **no education-sector rules**; Mexico's **SEP / ANUIES / Observatorio IA** recommendations are 🔴 **non-binding**; the **IDB**'s **ILIA** index covers 19 countries | 🟢 **Yes — the faculty/student split is new.** Two instruments, two methods, same conclusion: **adoption far ahead of governance** |

🟢 **The durable conclusion survives a sixth re-check and is now corroborated
rather than single-sourced:** LATAM pairs the region's highest measured adoption
with the least scaffolding — **87 % using AI against 26 % with a framework**
(IESALC), **92 % / 79 % engaging against ~30 % with a published policy** (DEC ×
Tec de Monterrey). 🔵 Two separate instruments, built by different
organisations, measuring different populations, producing the same **~60-point
gap between use and governance**. That gap is the most reliable single number on
this page.

🔴 **Global market size got worse, not better, as evidence.** Three firms, same
sector, overlapping years:

| Firm | Figure | Implied 2026 | CAGR claimed |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The Business Research Company** | $7.52B (2025) → **$10.6B (2026)** | **$10.6B** | 40.9 % |
| **IMARC** | $6.4B (2025) → $79.6B (2034) | ~$8.4B (interpolated) | 31.35 % |
| **Persistence Market Research** | **$3.7B (2026)** → $14.1B (2033) | **$3.7B** | 21 % |

🔴 **The high and low 2026 figures differ by 2.9×**, and all three are
vendor-sponsored. 🟢 **The band is the honest answer** — *$3.7B–$10.6B for 2026,
21–41 % CAGR* — and it should be quoted as a band in any client material, never
as a point. 🔵 One adoption figure from the same sweep is worth more than all
three: **teacher AI professional development went from 29 % (early 2024) to 50 %
(late 2025)**, and **K-12 accounts for 45.62 % of adoption** against higher
education being the largest *revenue* segment — the two halves of the sector
pull in opposite directions on where the money and the users are.

## 🆕 `p534` — three load-bearing prose claims restored, because a gate finally ran

🔴 **`make gate-prose-claims` — run for the first time in at least eleven passes
— reports **eleven** load-bearing claims as `CLAIM-LOST` or `ARCHIVE-ONLY`:
present in this base's append-only history, **absent from the curated file that
is supposed to carry them.** Three of the eleven belong to this file and are
restored below with their evidence re-read this pass. 🔵 The other eight are
listed, unrestored, in [`open-gaps.md`](open-gaps.md) — restoring a claim
from memory is how a KB acquires confident errors, and eight of them turn on
security advisories and jurisdiction holdings `p534` did not verify.

- 🔴 **EMEA: there is still `no EMEA adoption rate` on this base.** Thirteen
  passes of declared gap, and `p534`'s own leg returned zero education-specific
  results for the second consecutive pass. 🔵 Written here in the file that
  carries the regional picture, because an unstated gap is indistinguishable
  from coverage — which is the entire reason the claim register exists.
- 🟢 **LATAM: the Digital Education Council's *AI in Higher Education LATAM
  Survey 2026* rests on `30,000+ responses` from **29** higher-education
  institutions.** Re-verified this pass: **7,319 faculty responses** are
  confirmed on the DEC's own pages; 🟡 the complementary **22,941 student**
  figure appears only in a LinkedIn post by one of the authors and is carried as
  *single-sourced*. 🔵 This population is why the LATAM numbers on this page
  outrank every other region's evidence here: 30k responses with a published
  method against roundups that do not name their data.
- 🟢 **LATAM: ~30 % of universities have a `published AI-use policy` against
  92 % student engagement**, and the contrast *is* the pitch — the bottleneck is
  **governance, not adoption or capability**. 🟢 Corroborated this pass by a
  second, independent instrument: UNESCO **IESALC** finds **26 %** of 200
  institutions across 19 countries hold any formal framework while **87 %** use
  AI in at least one area. 🆕 Two further DEC figures worth carrying: **94 % of
  LATAM faculty expect to use AI in future teaching**, and **65 % of students
  are concerned AI makes learning too shallow** — demand and distrust in the
  same population, which is what a governance deliverable actually has to
  answer.

## 🆕 `p534` — the APAC regulatory holdings, restored first-hand and **one of them corrected**

🔴 Three further claims `make gate-prose-claims` reported missing from this file
are APAC regulatory holdings. 🟢 **Re-verified this pass rather than restored
from memory — and the verification changed one of them.**

**🟢 Singapore — `SG-AGENTIC-FIRST`.** IMDA's **Model AI Governance Framework
for Agentic AI** was launched by Minister Josephine Teo at the World Economic
Forum on **22 January 2026**, and is described by the government and by multiple
law firms as the **world's first governance framework built specifically for
agentic AI** — systems that plan, reason and act autonomously. 🔵 It rests on
**four dimensions**: bound the risks up front, make humans *meaningfully*
accountable, implement technical controls and processes, and enable end-user
responsibility. 🟢 **Those four axes are the ones every recipe in
[`../compose/patterns.md`](../compose/patterns.md) has to defend**, which is why
this claim is load-bearing rather than background. 🔴 **It is explicitly
voluntary** — where the EU, US, Korea and Vietnam bind by statute, Singapore
deliberately does not — so it is the artefact to *show*, not a rule to comply
with. 🟡 The "world's first" label is the framing of its own publishers and this
pass did not independently establish that no earlier agent-specific framework
exists.

**🟡 Vietnam — `VN-CORPUS-TRIGGER`, restored with a condition the register
omitted.** **Decision No. 33/2026/QD-TTg** (dated **30 June 2026**, effective
**15 August 2026**) lists **46** high-risk AI systems across six sectors; its
education appendix names **three**: self-learning content built from
`uncontrolled data sources`; automated **assessment or ranking** of learners;
and **biometric** monitoring of learner behaviour. 🔴 **The correction:** this
base has carried the first category as making *"a RAG tutor over an unvetted
corpus high-risk on the retrieval design alone."* The official text qualifies
it — the category applies **where the system is deployed at large scale, with
potential for systemic risk or widespread impact on learners**. 🟢 So the
holding survives and is **narrower than this base stated**: a pilot is not
automatically high-risk, a national rollout is, and *"not all AI systems used in
these sectors will automatically be classified as high-risk."* 🔵 The
consequence for a design is unchanged and still unusual: **a tutor with no
grading feature anywhere in it can be high-risk on its retrieval design**, and
an EU-conformant build has **no corpus-provenance artefact** to show for it.

**🟢 Vietnam — `VN-EDU-DEADLINE`.** The conditional is the whole value, so it is
stated as a conditional: education, healthcare and financial systems **already
in operation before 15 August 2026** have an extended grace period and must
comply by **`1 Sep 2027`**. 🔴 **Everything else — including anything deployed
in the six months after the Decision — is due `1 March 2027`.** 🔵 For an
engagement that means the **deployment date decides the deadline**, and a
greenfield build started now inherits the *earlier* one.

Plus a **European Commission** report (Sept 2026): **29.7%** of EU teachers have had any CPD on using AI in teaching, **53%** have had none *and report no strong need for it*, and **~two-thirds** of EU education systems now have an AI strategy or policy framework. An EC teacher survey (**n=1 130**, 54% secondary) reports **73%** using AI for lesson planning and **67%** for assessment preparation — 🔴 **self-selected, explicitly not representative** | 🟢 **`Gap 534-F` ADVANCED, and not by a new query — by a *different instrument class*, exactly as the gap prescribed.** Twelve passes asked for "EMEA adoption" and got EU regulation; this pass asked the survey that already produced the LATAM rows. 🔴 **Not closed**: `www.oecd.org` and `school-education.ec.europa.eu` are both **`ENOTFOUND`** from this session, so **no figure here was read from its primary** |
| **APAC** | 🟢 **The same instrument fills the APAC blank this file has recorded twice**: **Singapore ~75%** of teachers using AI in their work — the joint highest of any TALIS 2024 system — against 🔴 **Japan <20%**, the joint lowest | 🟢 **The two-pass "no APAC adoption rate exists in any result" blank is FILLED**, same caveat: search-backend, primary unread. 🔵 The **55-point spread inside one region** is the finding — "APAC adoption" is not a number |
| **North America** | 🟡 **The regulatory picture is saturated as recorded** (134 bills / 31 states, Gallup 60%, the four states mandating district policy). 🟢 **New, and it is about *evidence* rather than policy**: a coalition reported as **140+ organisations** (Alliance for Learning Innovation) published a report **2026-10-15** asking Congress and the administration to fund research into whether AI tools actually improve learning; the review behind it is reported as finding that of **800+ K-12 AI studies**, only **~20 produced strong causal evidence of learning impact** (attributed to the Stanford Accelerator for Learning) | 🟢 **New to this base** — nothing matching `Stanford Accelerator`, `Alliance for Learning Innovation` or that 800/20 ratio appears anywhere in it. 🔴 **Every host carrying it is `ENOTFOUND`** (`edweek.org`, `acceleratelearning.stanford.edu`, `aasa.org`), so it is recorded as a **single-channel claim** and drives [`trends.md`](trends.md) Trend 21 |
| **LATAM** | 🔴 **Nothing new.** The leg returned the holdings this base already has first-hand or near-first-hand: UNESCO IESALC (200 institutions / 19 countries, 87% using AI, 74% in teaching and learning, 26% with a formal strategy), the LAC Observatory launched April 2026 at ECLAC, TALIS 2024 (Brazil 56 / Chile 55 / Colombia 53 / Costa Rica 52), Ceibal, CENIA's "regional public investment six times below the global average", and the Universidad de los Andes count of **716 AI regulatory instruments across 23 countries** | 🔴 **Saturated, and said so rather than re-reported as new.** 🟢 One structural note: LATAM is now the **best-evidenced region on this base** and EMEA the worst, which inverts the assumption the KB was started on |

### 🔴 The money leg, and it points the other way from the market-size band

🟢 **New to this base this pass**, and it belongs beside the four-firm size band above
because it contradicts the mood that band creates:

- **EdTech venture funding is reported down ~89%** from its peak, with the decline
  continuing through the first half of 2026 — while the *AI-in-education* segment is
  the thing every forecast in this file projects at **25–45% CAGR**.
- **Reach Capital** closed a **$265M** fund to back AI applications across learning,
  health and work.
- **IXL Learning acquired NoRedInk** — reported as its **14th acquisition since 2018**.
- **UK**: a package reported at **£600m+** for AI, whose central education commitment is
  a **UK Advanced Chip Design Academy** backed by **£18m** — 🔵 note the shape, because
  it is an EMEA *skills-and-silicon* bet, not a classroom bet.

🔴 **Read together, these say the buyer is consolidating, not expanding.** Capital is
scarce, incumbents are absorbing products, and the public money in EMEA is going to
chip-design skills. 🟢 **For a studio this is good news and the market-size band is
not**: a client in a consolidating sector buys *integration into what they already run*
— which is what this base's permissive MCP/LTI shelf is for — rather than a new platform.
🔴 **All four bullets are single-channel** (`worldassessmentcouncil.org`,
`edtechinnovationhub.com` both `ENOTFOUND`). → `Gap 535-D`.

Thirteen addresses, **twelve permissively licensed** (eleven MIT, one Apache-2.0) and one
split-grant, each reaching a **named national or institutional system of record**: SIASE,
SAES, SIA, SUAP, BNCC, WebUntis, Schulmanager, PowerSchool, Ed-Fi. 🟢 **This is
`Trend 41`'s adapter inversion with a price list attached.** None of these is a platform
to fork; each is the thin, permissive, usually single-author bridge between an agent and a
system the client does not control — and **every one of them is a maintenance liability
the client inherits** (two are already stale, three have a single author and zero forks).

🔴 **`Mcp-Brasil/mcp-brasil` is the row that should not have waited.** 1 840 stars,
287 forks, MIT, pushed on the day of this read, exposing **70 Brazilian public-sector
APIs** through one MCP server — and it was named in a sentence on this page and filed in
no table row on this base. 🟢 It is the single most load-bearing LATAM integration
asset here, and `P537-A` is built on it.

## 🆕 `p539` — the four regional legs re-run: **demand saturated for an eighth pass; the regulatory calendar is now the engagement driver**

🔴 **All four regional legs returned no new demand-side number this base did not
already hold.** That is the eighth consecutive pass to report it and it is no
longer worth re-litigating: the *demand* question for education AI is answered,
and the legs are now worth running for **regulation** and **supply**, which is
where all four moved this pass.

🟢 **What did move: every region now has a dated compliance deadline**, and they
do not line up. That is the engagement driver a studio can sell against, because
it converts "AI governance" from a posture into a date.

| Region | The dated instrument | Date | What it forces on an education build |
|---|---|---|---|
| **North America** | Ohio — districts must have adopted an AI-use policy | **1 Jul 2026 (past)** | A policy exists to comply with; the gap is enforcement tooling, not drafting |
| **North America** | ADA web-accessibility update for large public institutions | **Apr 2026 (past)** | Accessibility is now a compliance surface, not a feature — and it is the one AI demonstrably helps with (captioning, alt text, reading level) |
| **North America** | Oklahoma S.B. 1734 — written district AI policy | **before the 2027–28 school year** | 🟡 A bill, not law at the time of reading |
| **EMEA** | EU AI Act Annex III high-risk obligations, standalone systems | 🔴 **2 Dec 2027** | Conformity assessment **and** registration in the EU database **before market** |
| **EMEA** | the same, for systems embedded in a regulated product | 🔴 **Aug 2028** | |
| **APAC** | South Korea AI Basic Act in force | **22 Jan 2026 (past)** | MSIT has signalled 2026 is effectively a pilot year with limited enforcement; decrees still being finalised |
| **APAC** | Vietnam's AI law, risk-based, **education named in the high-risk list** | **1 Mar 2026 (past)** | Automated assessment and behavioural uses are high-risk in one of six named sectors |
| **LATAM** | 🔴 **no regional deadline** | — | 716 instruments across 23 countries and no binding education-specific date; see §LATAM |

⚠️ **The EU dates carry a source caveat and it is not resolved.** One source
attributes the delay to a *Digital Omnibus* amendment effective July 2026,
published in the Official Journal on **24 July 2026 as Regulation (EU)
2026/1744**, and a second says a political agreement postponed standalone
high-risk obligations to **2 Dec 2027**. The two are consistent in substance and
**internally inconsistent on dating**, and both are secondary sources. 🔴 **Do
not put either citation in front of a client without reading the Official
Journal entry first.** What is NOT in doubt, because it is in the Act itself:
education systems that evaluate learners, assign them to programmes, detect
prohibited behaviour during assessment, or influence access to qualifications
are **Annex III high-risk**.

🔵 **The OECD's 2026 Digital Education Outlook is the first institutional source
this base has seen state the thesis it has been filing evidence for:** move
beyond general-purpose AI tools toward **purpose-built educational AI**. Every
shelf row anchored to a named standard — BNCC, ENEM, the National Curriculum for
England, ESCO, xAPI — is an instance of exactly that, and the argument now has a
citation that is not this base's own prose.

## 🆕 `p540` — the four regional legs re-run: **demand saturated for a ninth pass, and LATAM's real gap turns out not to be adoption at all**

🔴 **Ninth consecutive pass with no new demand-side number.** Reported, not
re-litigated. The legs are run for **regulation** and **supply**, and this pass
all four moved on regulation while **none** returned permissive supply —
everything new on the supply side this pass came from gating this base's own
backlog, not from a regional search. 🔵 That is itself a finding about the
instrument: **regional search legs are now a regulation instrument, and the
supply instrument is the backlog gate.**

### 🟢 The single most useful number this pass found, and it reframes the LATAM pitch

UNESCO IESALC surveyed **200 higher-education institutions across 19 countries**
in Latin America and the Caribbean:

| What | Share |
|---|---|
| Using AI in at least one area | 🟢 **87 %** |
| Using AI in teaching and learning | 🟢 **74 %** |
| Holding a **formal AI strategy** | 🔴 **26 %** |
| **Evaluating what their AI use delivers** | 🔴 **9 %** |

🔵 **LATAM's gap is not adoption — adoption is near-universal. The gap is
evidence.** Ninety-one percent of institutions that have already deployed AI
cannot say whether it worked. 🟢 **That is a far better engagement shape than
"help us adopt AI", because the buyer already spent the money and now has to
defend it**, and it lands directly on the efficacy layer `p535` filed and on
this pass's [`EducationalTestingService/skll`](https://github.com/EducationalTestingService/skll)
row — a BSD-3-Clause experiment runner from the organisation behind TOEFL and
the GRE. The supply for the measurement engagement is already on this base's
shelves; what was missing was the demand statistic, and it is now here.

🟡 Corroborating, same region, different instrument: **Brazil — 53 % of school
administrators use AI, 22 % report their school has guidelines on its use**
(Cetic.br). The strategy-to-use ratio holds at school level as well as
university level.

### 🟢 Regional legs — what each returned, including the blanks

| Region | 🟢 New this pass | 🔴 Returned nothing on |
|---|---|---|
| **North America** | **H.R. 8747**, the *K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026*, advanced out of the House Education and Workforce Committee at a **21 July markup**, largely on party lines — it would amend ESEA to let federal funds buy AI curricula. **Maryland** gives all 24 districts **120 days from MSDE guidance** to adopt aligned policies. **Utah** mandates a grade 7-or-8 digital-skills course including AI literacy. A tracker counts **134 AI-in-education bills across 31 states** in the 2026 session. | 🔴 No new supply. 🔴 **Canada returned almost nothing again** — the only hit was workplace AI rules under the *Working for Workers Four Act, 2024* and pending Ontario AI-hiring disclosure, neither education-specific. Canada has now been a blank on this leg for several passes and should be searched by name, not as part of "North America". |
| **EMEA** | 🟢 **A second, independent source for the EU high-risk delay** that `p539` flagged as unresolved: the *Digital Omnibus on AI*, **Regulation (EU) 2026/1744**, moving Annex III high-risk education obligations to **December 2027**. 🟢 **And two duties that are already in force and were not on this page:** the **AI-literacy duty since February 2025** for every school that uses AI, and the **ban on emotion recognition in education**, also since February 2025. 🟢 The **European Commission and OECD published a joint AI-literacy framework for primary and secondary education on 18 June 2026.** | 🔴 No new supply. 🔴 No market-size or vendor-share study for EMEA education AI — a blank this base has carried for many passes and did not fill. |
| **APAC** | 🟢 **The detail that makes Vietnam's law actionable:** its transition periods are **12 months generally, but 18 months for health care, education and finance** — so education systems already in operation at commencement have until roughly **1 September 2027**. 🟡 South Korea's AI Basic Act (in force 22 Jan 2026) — MSIT decrees still being finalised, consistent with what this page already holds. 🟢 **Australia's National AI Plan (December 2025) deliberately relies on existing law rather than a standalone AI act**, and Japan continues on voluntary guidelines — so APAC is **not** converging on one model. Ipsos *Education Monitor 2026*: support for banning AI in schools **rose** in Japan, Australia and South Korea and **fell** in Indonesia. | 🔴 No new supply. 🔴 **India returned nothing**, despite being named a dominant market by the one market report the leg surfaced — a conspicuous blank given its share of global education demand. |
| **LATAM** | 🟢 The **UNESCO IESALC** figures above. 🟢 **The Observatory on Artificial Intelligence in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean launched 14 April 2026** — UNESCO-led, with **CAF**, **CENIA** (Chile), **Cetic.br** (Brazil), **ECLAC**, **Fundación Ceibal** (Uruguay), **Fundación Santillana**, **Tecnológico de Monterrey**, **ProFuturo**, **Universidad del Desarrollo** and **IRCAI**. That is a named, funded, multi-country counterpart list — the thing a regional engagement needs and this page did not have. 🟡 Uruguay via Ceibal: **75 % of public-school teachers** report using these tools. | 🔴 No new supply from the search leg — but see `Gap 540-B`: this pass **measured** that the grant classifier under-grades Portuguese-language licence files, so some share of LATAM's apparent supply blank is **instrument, not absence**. That is the first time this base can say that with a measurement behind it. |

### 🔴 One status conflict this pass could not settle, flagged rather than merged

`p539` recorded **Oklahoma S.B. 1734** (written district AI policy before the
2027-28 school year) as 🟡 *"a bill, not law at the time of reading."* This
pass's source describes it as enacted, and adds a provision `p539` does not
carry: that AI **may not be the primary basis for grading, discipline,
placement or other high-stakes decisions**. 🔴 **Both are secondary sources and
this pass did not read the enrolled text.** The substantive provision matters
more than the status — it is a direct constraint on automated-assessment
architecture — so it is recorded here with the conflict visible. 🔵 **Do not
put the status in front of a client without reading the Oklahoma legislature's
own record.**

### 🔵 What this means for the regional engagement shapes

- **North America** — the compliance surface is **districts drafting and then
  enforcing policy**, 134 bills deep and with no federal floor yet. The product
  is policy-conformance tooling over an existing LMS, not a tutor.
- **EMEA** — the two duties already in force (**AI literacy**, **emotion-recognition
  ban**) are enforceable *today*, while the high-risk obligations are 2027.
  Schools are **deployers** and stay responsible for third-party tools, so the
  near-term sale is a deployer-side AI inventory and literacy programme, and
  the 2027 work is conformity assessment.
- **APAC** — no single model, so **jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction** work. Vietnam's
  18-month education carve-out is the nearest hard date with a named sector.
- **LATAM** — **not an adoption engagement, an evaluation engagement.** 87 %
  deployed, 9 % measuring. Lead with efficacy measurement and the counterpart
  list the new UNESCO Observatory supplies.

## 🆕 `p542` — the four regional legs re-run: **"saturated" was the wrong word for ten passes**

**The four mandated regional legs were run in the mandated form, and then the
supply legs were re-run in eight further languages.** 🔴 **The demand side
restated almost everything, as it has since `p532`** — the NA 36 % share, the
`$169 M` Q1 2026 federal commitment, the 134-bills-in-31-states tracker, the
EU AI Act Article 50 date, Latam-GPT, UNESCO IESALC, the OECD *Digital
Education Outlook*. 🟢 **But grading the claims against the committed tree
rather than against memory gives a different verdict than "saturated":**

| | Count | What it means |
|---|---|---|
| Claims already **filed** on a live page | — | the genuinely saturated part |
| Claims held **only in trending history** | **6** | known, measured, **never filed** — `Gap 529-A` on the intel side |
| Claims **absent from this base entirely** | **5** | filed below, this pass |

🔵 **The six trending-only claims:** Singapore's *"Four Learns"* (COS,
March 2026); the EC + OECD *Empowering Learners for the Age of AI* framework
(18 June 2026); Council of the EU `ST-9003-2026` (11 May 2026); Ireland's
Oide guidance; `moe.gov.sg` and CSET's translation of China's 2025 GenAI
school guide; Europe EdTech 200 (2026) and OpenAI × IIT Madras.
🟢 **The five absent ones:** Singapore's **AIEd Framework** and **EdTech
Masterplan 2030**; China MOE's **2026 digital-education priorities** and the
**AI + Education Action Plan** (2 April 2026, five departments); the **ASEAN
Guide on AI Governance and Ethics**; the IDB **fAIr Tech Radar** (November
2025); and **FIPSE**, the award vehicle behind the `$169 M` figure this base
has carried as a bare number since `P515`.

🔴 **So the honest restatement: the regional legs are not saturated — the
promotion step is.** Ten passes have reported "demand saturated" on the
strength of legs that were returning material this base had never filed.
**"Already held" and "already filed" are different verdicts, and only the
second one licenses a pass to stop looking.**

### ⚠️ A verification limit that applies to every claim filed below

**None of the five absent claims could be verified at its primary source from
this environment.** `education.ec.europa.eu` **does not resolve** here, as
`gitlink.org.cn` does not; the ministry and multilateral-bank domains were not
reachable. 🟢 **Each claim below is therefore attributed to the secondary
source that carried it and marked `unverified-at-primary`.** That is a real
and stated limit: these are leads a consultant can act on by reading the
primary document themselves, **not facts this base has measured** — which is a
different standard from the repository rows, every one of which was read
first-hand this pass.

## 🆕 `p543` — the four regional legs re-run: **the supply side has a licence problem, and it is regional**

**Thirty-one claims off the mandated English legs and the four regional legs,
graded by instrument rather than by memory**
([`../ingest/claim-role.sh`](../ingest/claim-role.sh)): **22 `FILED`,
6 `HISTORY`, 3 `ABSENT`.** Demand is saturated for an eleventh pass. 🟢 **What
is *not* saturated, and what this pass can state for the first time with
numbers, is that the regions differ in whether their domestic education supply
is LICENSED at all.**

| Leg | What it returned that this base did not hold | Licensed? |
|---|---|---|
| **North America** | Nothing absent. Two claims promoted out of history: **Berkeley Law's exam ban** and **Chicago's high-stakes-decision clause**. | — |
| **EMEA** | **Two licensed Turkish assets** — `captainmgc/tubitak-skills` (MIT) and `kmkarakaya/btk_rag_bot_egitimi` (MIT, a **state ICT authority's** course) — plus a declared Turkish **localisation** of a held English curriculum. | 🟢 **Yes, all three MIT.** |
| **APAC** | **Three domestic Vietnamese education agents**, all absent, all substantive. | 🔴 **No — zero grants on three channels.** |
| **LATAM** | 🔴 **Nothing absent on either demand or supply.** Said plainly rather than left blank: the leg returned the UNESCO IESALC study, the Andes 716-instrument count, Ceibal, Cetic.br, CENIA and Tec de Monterrey — **every one already filed here.** One claim promoted out of history: the **Digital Education Council LATAM survey**. |

🔵 **The finding is the asymmetry, and it changes how a regional engagement is
scoped.** Türkiye's domestic education supply arrives **permissively licensed
and institutionally anchored** — a state authority's course, a national
funder's proposal workflow. Vietnam's arrives **unlicensed**: three real,
well-built, education-specific agents that an engagement cannot legally
build on. 🟢 **For APAC that converts a supply finding into a
service offer — licensing, governance and productisation of domestic
prototypes — rather than a component to adopt.** 🔴 **And it means a
"what open source exists here" answer is incomplete without the grant: by
artefact count Vietnam out-produced Türkiye on this pass; by usable artefact
count it produced nothing.**

### ⚠️ The verification limit on every claim in this section, stated precisely

`p542` reported that five claims *"could not be verified at their primary
sources"*, naming `education.ec.europa.eu` as unresolvable **to `curl`**.
🟢 **`p543` retested on the other channel — the asymmetric one `p534` proved
reads GitHub pages that `curl` cannot — and it fails there too**, as does
`www.iesalc.unesco.org`. 🔵 **These hosts are outside the session's allowed
network domains: a DNS allowlist, not a proxy rule and not a channel artefact.**
🔴 **The remedy is a network-policy change** — the same class as `Gap 542-D`'s
Chinese forges. **Every European Commission and UNESCO IESALC figure on this
base is therefore secondary-attributed by necessity, and says so on its row.**

| `ABSENT` claim this pass filed or refused | Disposition |
|---|---|
| **Singapore — MAS AI Risk Management Guidelines** (consultation, 2026) | 🔴 **Refused on sector.** MAS is the **Monetary Authority** of Singapore, a *financial* regulator; the leg returned a finance instrument under an education query. 🔵 Singapore's genuine education-sector gap remains `p542`'s: the **AIEd Framework** and **EdTech Masterplan 2030**, still absent. |
| **China — penalties to ¥50 M** under its generative-AI framework (registration + content labelling) | 🟡 **Filed, attributed to its secondary source, marked UNVERIFIED at primary.** General-AI instrument with an education consequence, not an education instrument. |
| **eduTrac SIS** | 🟢 Filed to [`../verticals/solutions.md`](../verticals/solutions.md) — **GPL-3.0, archived 2026-08-12**, refuting the "MIT" claim that carried it. |
